For the benefit of all lovers of natural medicine <term>Artzey</term> <note>The archaic German word "Artzey" (Arznei) refers broadly to the "art of healing" or medicinal preparations.</note> and those who have a passion for alchemy: I have now brought to light—for the common good—this work, which was formerly published (under the name of Basilius Valentinus) in a rough and unpolished state. These explanations, which I previously wrote concerning this subject, have now reached their third edition. As the true author, I have diligently improved and expanded them, and arranged them in a new order. They are presented in three languages—Latin, French, and German—so that one may see how, to the best of my humble ability, I have brought the truth out of midnight darkness into the light of day, like the sun breaking through thick clouds. To God alone, however, belongs the glory.

# Gentle Reader.

Among all lovers of the excellent, yet no less difficult and secret art and science of **alchemy**, the writings which have come to light under the name of **Basilius Valentinus** <note>Basilius Valentinus is a legendary 15th-century figure, purportedly a Benedictine monk, whose influential alchemical texts were actually published much later, starting around 1600.</note> have for a long time been held in such high regard and value—indeed, esteemed as so reliable—that it is believed they, above many other old and new **chemical** books, could serve as a compass and a true **guide** for those who possess an earnest desire to grasp and carry out something thorough and useful in the most important **spagyric** <term>spagyric</term> <note>Derived from the Greek words for "to separate" and "to combine," spagyric refers to the alchemical process of breaking down plants or minerals and then recombining them to create more potent medicinal tinctures.</note> **processes** and labors.

And this is undoubtedly the reason why the works of our Basilius have already been published several times, not only in the **German language** according to the original, but also translated into other European languages, thereby making this author widely known and beloved across many nations far and wide. Whether, however, this author’s genuine and

# Preface.

The goals toward which he directed his investigation of <term>Antimonium</term> <note>original: "Antimonium oder Spießglas"; antimony, a semi-metal used both in metallurgy and medicine</note> can be sufficiently understood partly from his own preface (which shall be added hereafter for that reason) and partly from the remarks shared by him. However, this work undertaken by him will seem all the less strange to anyone the better known it is that, since the time of <term>Basilius</term> <note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings popularized antimony</note>—who was the first to break the path in this matter—antimony has been regarded by many devotees of the <term>spagyric art</term> <note>original: "ſpagyriſchen Kunſt"; the alchemical art of separating and recombining plant or mineral extracts for medicine</note> as such a unique substance and essence. In its inner constitution and almost inexhaustible power, it has no equal among the other known minerals and metals.

And certainly, it is known nowadays how to manufacture as many good, proven medicines from antimony—suitable for both internal and external use—as one is otherwise able to produce from any single thing in the threefold kingdom of nature <note>The "threefold kingdom" refers to the traditional division of Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral</note>. Perhaps in time, even more will be discovered that may serve not only for medicine but also for secret philosophical works with greater certainty than has happened until now. For this reason, some devotees of alchemy still promise themselves uncommon and great things from antimony because of its extraordinary property and virtue—by which it makes all gold pure and fine—as well as because of the <term>fixed sulfur of gold</term> <note>original: "fixen Gold-Schwefels"; a theoretical alchemical principle believed to be the "soul" or coloring agent of gold</note> hidden within it; as can be seen among many other special and

# Preface

)( )( 3

Among the remarkable things found in the inaugural dissertation *On Universal Medicine* <note>original: "de Medicina universali"</note> held at Jena in the year 1679 by Adam Gottlob Berlich, there are various points that well deserve to be read. This is especially true in Chapter XII, page 40 and following, where the author cites several curious experiments he performed. From these, the extraordinary effects of antimony <note>original: "Antimonii"</note> in the three kingdoms of nature <note>Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral</note> can be recognized to some extent, serving as a preliminary proof.

The same is clearly evident from the spagyric <term>Spagyric: A term from Paracelsus referring to the art of separating and recombining natural substances to create potent medicines.</term> processes undertaken with antimony by our colleague Kerckring. In these, he always aimed for something greater and more significant. Although he did not fully achieve this goal—performing many experiments in vain, repeating them frequently, and (as he himself writes) having to sacrifice great effort and several thousand guilders over many years—he nevertheless learned, saw, and experienced much. This knowledge could afterwards serve both himself and others usefully by saving time and unnecessary expense. Not to mention that he was indeed granted some excellent and uncommon results, which another person—who might not have so much time and money to spend—could hardly hope to obtain, unless they are shown the right path through closer and more certain instruction from someone already practiced and experienced in these matters. Now, to demonstrate this as much as possible within the work itself, and to those devoted to secret chemistry <note>original: "geheimen Chymie," referring to alchemy</note>...

<summary>The author cites the academic work of Adam Gottlob Berlich and the costly experiments of Theodor Kerckring to justify the study of antimony's universal medicinal properties. He emphasizes that sharing this specialized knowledge saves future researchers from wasted time and financial ruin.</summary>

<keywords>Antimony, Adam Gottlob Berlich, Theodor Kerckring, Spagyric medicine, Alchemy, Universal Medicine, Jena, Scientific experimentation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface, likely written by an editor or publisher, discussing the motivations of Theodor Kerckring in publishing his commentaries on Basil Valentine.</meta>

# Preface

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

To offer a helping hand to those who are advanced but still somewhat unpracticed beginners in these matters, our Kerckring did not wish to keep his discoveries—though they were costly and came at a considerable expense to him—to himself alone. Rather, he considered it an honor if others, through his generous sharing of various <term>spagyric</term> <note>Spagyric refers to alchemical preparation, specifically the process of separating and recombining the essential components of a substance to create medicine.</note> secrets, might find and strike the right path toward obtaining them. To this end, he deemed it best to take as much as seemed useful from what had become known to him through the experiments made with <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonio"; a metallic element central to alchemical research and medicine in the 17th century.</note> and to arrange these into certain observations. He placed these in the appropriate locations beneath the text of the *Triumphal Chariot of Basil* <note>Referring to "The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony" by Basil Valentine, a foundational alchemical text.</note>, allowing both to be published together in Latin, so that such a writing might benefit many other nations among those who are knowledgeable in the learned language <note>Latin was the universal language of science and scholarship in Europe at this time.</note>. There is also no reason to doubt that our author's intended purpose and the effort and expense he applied have been well received; this can be sufficiently gathered, among other things, from the successful sale of his treatise, of which copies have currently become so rare that they are almost nowhere to be found in bookshops anymore. Not to mention that this little book, because of its important content, has been translated by foreigners and was published for the second time by a curious Englishman in the year 1678. In that English preface, both the usefulness of <term>Antimony</term> and the experiments made with it by our Kerckring were simultaneously praised.

<summary>The author explains that Theodor Kerckring published his expensive alchemical discoveries regarding antimony to assist others, choosing to write in Latin for an international audience. The text notes the high demand for the work and its subsequent translation into English due to its scientific value.</summary>

<keywords>Theodor Kerckring, Antimony, Basil Valentine, alchemy, spagyric secrets, 17th-century publishing, Latin scholarship, Triumphal Chariot of Antimony</keywords>

can be praised in no small measure. # Preface.

...cite [and] include <note>original: "anführen / und mit einfliessen lassen"</note> false and incorrect—indeed, even absurd—causes which, in this day and age, are no longer accepted as valid, now that we seek and demand more certain, more general principles <note>original: "Principia"</note> or fundamental laws in **Natural Science** <term>Natur-Wissenschafft: the study of the physical world, encompassing physics, chemistry, and biology</term> that serve to provide concise conclusions. For such a speculative deficiency does not diminish the utility of things that prove themselves in **Practice** <note>original: "Praxi"</note> in the slightest; just as it does not hinder an experienced **Pilot** or helmsman from using the **Sea-Compass** readily and correctly, even if he cannot indicate or explain the actual causes of that force which constantly turns and directs the **Magnetic Needle** toward the **Polar Point**.

In such matters, everything depends primarily on **Experience** <note>original: "Erfahrung"</note>; as long as this is reliable, the **Theory** will eventually follow. Besides, it should not be particularly difficult for an intelligent and practiced **Natural Researcher** to occasionally elucidate this or that **Experiment**—which is correct in its actual execution—through better and more probable causes. In this regard, every impartial lover of truth who is not ignorant of these matters will gladly admit that even according to today’s newest teaching methods customary in **Natural Science**—however much one has endeavored to arrange them according to **Metaphysical** and **Mathematical** principles joined together—there is also a multitude of all kinds of newly and ingeniously devised and manufactured **Instruments** and **Machines**,

# Preface

used it—and certainly not without some benefit—nevertheless, many things occur here and there in natural matters whose true and essential nature cannot be fully explored down to the innermost depths, much less precisely defined or measured. Instead, they will perhaps remain hidden for a long time yet in the well of **Democritus** <note>A reference to the ancient Greek philosopher Democritus, who famously stated that "truth lies at the bottom of a well," implying it is difficult to uncover.</note>.

The aforementioned sympathetic and magnetic power can testify to this to a considerable extent; for as well-known as it has been for several hundred years according to its effects, its actual cause has remained just as unknown until now. What **Descartes** <note>original: "Cartesius"</note>, and others after him following the same stroke, reason about it, appears—in the judgment of famous **physicists** <note>term: Physicorum (natural philosophers or scientists)</note> who look for sufficient proof—to be far too forced and fabricated. Thus, anyone who would allow themselves to be fed and satisfied with such empty mental images would have a poor appetite for the solid nourishment of thorough knowledge. To be sure, people have continued to research further into this and have recently discovered something more of the magnetic power; of which, among others, the **History** and **Memoirs of the Royal Academy of Sciences** in **Paris** contain some curious reports and **observations**. # Preface.

It would not be unprofitable if someone were to thoroughly investigate these matters and clearly demonstrate whether, or to what extent, they are important and capable enough to prove what is intended through valid and concise conclusions. However, anyone who otherwise reads with reflection the writings of those authors <note>original: "Autorum"</note> who possessed great knowledge and experience of minerals <term>Mineralien: naturally occurring inorganic substances</term> and metals, and who personally visits mines here and there, descends into the shafts, observes the subterranean works of nature and art as closely as possible, inquires with sensible and experienced miners about this or that, and otherwise notes everything well—such a person will see and hear various things that do not entirely agree with the doctrines of today's natural science, or for which the causes cannot be explained with sufficient probability. 

But it would require even far greater effort if a probable explanation were to be given—according to their causes and how such things could be possible—for that which is presented as correct and proven in the writings of famous spagyric <term>spagyrischen: an early term for alchemy, specifically the art of separating and recombining plant or mineral constituents</term> philosophers and artists <note>original: "Artisten"</note>. For example, the works of Basil Valentine <note>Basilius Valentinus: a legendary 15th-century alchemist, likely a pseudonym for Johann Thölde</note>, Paracelsus, and among the more recent, the Dutchman Goosen van Vreeswyk, who was well-experienced and inquisitive in both mining matters and secret chemistry <note>original: "Chymie"</note>, as well as others. For one need not even mention what these same men have written, both in general regarding their great philosophical work—which they call the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: "Stein der Weisen"</note>—and in particular regarding the preparation of...

# Preface

to the aforementioned Stone, belonging to and indissolubly to be united with one another, the philosophical **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphuri"; in alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible principle</note> and **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; the fluid, metallic principle</note>, to which incomparable properties and effects are ascribed. Thus, their **Oil of Vitriol** <note>original: "Oleum Vitrioli"; concentrated sulfuric acid</note> alone can serve as an example because of its quite singular nature; for although it is a fire concentrated to the highest degree, and eats through and destroys all dead and lifeless things, it is nevertheless said to neither attack nor injure the flesh or bones of a living human being, except where something decayed is found upon them. To such uncommon matters—which are difficult to investigate from their essential foundations—one might also reasonably add the various types of **Phosphorus** <note>original: "Phosphori"; substances that glow in the dark</note> and **Pyrophorus** <note>original: "Pyrophori"; substances that ignite spontaneously in air</note> newly produced through the help of Chemistry, though their preparation costs no small amount of time and effort. Their various wonder-worthy properties and powers can give a Natural Philosopher <note>original: "Natur-Forscher"; a scientist or seeker of nature's secrets</note>, who seeks to discover their true causes, enough work to do. One need only consider what the aforementioned **Dippel** <note>Johann Konrad Dippel (1673–1734), a famous and controversial alchemist and theologian</note> (whose singular knowledge and experience, not only in common but also in the more secret Chemistry, has already become sufficiently known) brings forward among other things regarding **Phosphorus** in the above-cited *Disquisition*; there he says that it possesses such an exceedingly great altering and dividing power that even the hardest metallic bodies, which otherwise withstand all common fire, are dissolved by its flame in a well-covered vessel and carried upward, but then, after the subsequent separation

He frequently stood directly over the melting crucible in which the **Antimony** <term>Antimonium: a metallic element often used in early modern medicine, though its safety was hotly debated.</term> was in flux, and with his mouth wide open, he strongly inhaled and drew into himself the thick and whitish smoke rising abundantly from it through deep breaths. # Preface.

I deemed it more useful to retain the text unchanged <note>The publisher is likely referring to the original German text of Basilius Valentinus</note> rather than creating a new German translation from Kerckring’s Latin version. However, Kerckring’s concisely written Latin marginalia or side-glosses have not been omitted; instead, they have been included everywhere, translated into German <note>Now translated here into English</note>. Furthermore, since the copper-engraved figures in the Latin edition provide no small ornament to the book, and additionally offer significant clarification to the text in several places, the publisher did not wish to fall short in this regard either. Instead, he had the aforementioned figures neatly re-engraved, which—as is evident to the eye—the artist’s hand has captured quite well. All of this gives fair reason to hope that the well-disposed reader will receive and accept this present first German edition of Kerckring’s *Notes on Basilius’s Triumphal Chariot* with as much pleasure as was intended in serving him by bringing this work to completion. And so much for the preliminary report.

Now, before the well-disposed reader hears from Kerckring in his own preface (to which reference was made in the preliminary report), it will not be displeasing to him to look upon and consider for a moment the Dedication which precedes it in order. This dedication is addressed to the so-called Society of the Rose-Cross <note>The Rosicrucians, a mysterious esoteric brotherhood</note>, whose members are personally very hidden, yet have been known everywhere for a long time through so many writings. Its content is quite obscure, and its true meaning is difficult enough to discern in the Latin, let alone to translate clearly into another language;

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, justifying the inclusion of the dedication which was omitted in foreign editions.</meta>

# Preface.

Perhaps this may well be the reason why this *Dedication* was left out by foreigners without a *translation* <note>original: "Version"</note>. For *Kerckring* <note>Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693), a Dutch anatomist and alchemist.</note> wrote only for the experts, of whom he believed that they would already understand what he intended to convey and suggest through his secret and *enigmatic* <note>original: "Ænigmatische"</note> style of writing. However, so that nothing of *Kerckring's* spagyric <term>spagyric: alchemical herbal medicine involving the separation and recombination of plant components</term> writings should be withheld from the German reader, the translator of these notes has spared no effort to provide and print this *Dedication* in German as well and clearly as he was able to grasp it from the *Latin*. He was all the more encouraged to do so after becoming aware—from the frequently cited *Foundations of Stahlian Chemistry, page 131, section 22* <note>Refers to Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), a famous chemist and physician</note>—that the *author* noted a singular *distillation* of *living mercury by itself* <note>original: "Mercurii vivi per se"</note> from this very *Dedication*. This is a secret that would hardly be noticed immediately by someone else who does not have such deep insight into these matters.

->To the<-
->Excellent, Highly Honored, Most Godly and<-

# Dedicatory Epistle

I have no hesitation in addressing you, most eminent men, whom I admire, esteem, and revere—indeed, almost worship—as unknown figures. For I know what you are, and I recognize what you have attained, even though I am entirely ignorant of who you are. And even if I did know, I would still address you by no other than an unknown name, nor dedicate these first-fruits of <term>Chemical Science</term><note>The author uses "Chymische Wissenschafft," referring to the art of alchemy and early chemistry as a unified sacred science.</note> under any other title, which I hereby do in all devotion. I expect or desire no other reward for this than that the rays of your favor might illuminate me, and that you might deign to further assist me with those principles so well known to you—recognizing me as one who (as you will perceive from this very work) is treading the right path that leads to the serene temples of Wisdom. 

At the same time, I ask that you would not refuse me that same service and favor which I here publicly show to the <term>Lovers of Chemistry</term><note>A common term for "Philalethes" or seekers of the Hermetic art.</note> regarding those things of which neither the author *Apollo* nor the president *Mercurius*<note>Apollo and Mercury here represent the solar and mercurial principles of alchemy, as well as the mythological patrons of revelation and secret knowledge.</note> permits one to write or speak of openly. I, who have already progressed somewhat further and—to speak in the words of our *Basilius*<note>Likely a reference to Basilius Valentinus, a legendary and influential alchemical author.</note>—have been promoted to a higher class, seek your guidance. It happened that—to speak with you in your own language—*Mercurius* came to me in my sleep and pulled me back as I was running swiftly along the ordinary path, saying: "Look at me, I who am not [clothed] in a common coat..."

# Dedicatory Writing

That this was no mere idle dream, however, I was assured by the bridal gift which I found in my hands; it consists indeed of gold, but is more precious to me than all the gold in the world. I intend to reveal this whenever the opportunity arises to know you face to face, and to hear and speak with you in freedom. Until such a time, so desired by me, fare well; and you, who are worthier and more pleasing to me than *Mercury* <note>The Roman god of commerce and communication, often associated with the quicksilver of the alchemists</note> or any *Genius* <note>A guardian spirit or guiding divine nature</note>, allow yourselves to be seen by me, your servant and client, out from the dark clouds wherein you remain hidden. # Dedicatory Writing

If it is so important to you, then you do not need my instruction; simply set out upon the path. Perhaps you will learn something through your mistakes—provided that your patience in searching and the money in your purse do not vanish first. These <note>referring to patience and financial resources</note> have been my own guides, to which I have added constant attentiveness and precise meditation <note>original: "Meditation," here meaning deep, focused contemplation</note> or reflection as companions. I have had no others, and you likewise need no others; therefore, just let me go my way. 

But if you wish to reach the place toward which your journey is directed in a shorter time, with lower expenses, and with less of the frustration that inevitably accompanies errors, then I shall be a not-unhelpful leader to you—or at least, should you fall into various wrong turnings <note>original: "Irrwege," meaning labyrinths or false paths</note>, I shall be a not-inconvenient signpost. Fare well; and if you give me your ear and make use of me, you will enjoy many advantages which cannot be further detailed here.

# Annotations.

Initially, I, Basilius Valentinus <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, though likely a pseudonym for Johann Thölde</note>, a brother of the sworn Order of Saint Benedict, set down a brief reminder to the kind-hearted reader regarding what is highly necessary for every <term>Spagyrist</term> <note>An alchemist who practices the "spagyric" art: the separation and recombination of natural substances to create medicines</note>—who, with the deepest sighs, is an eager seeker of the true Art—to consider and attend to, in the following manner: Before I [began] this <term>Libel</term> <note>From the Latin 'libellus', meaning a small book or formal treatise</note>

<summary>The title page and opening address of a famous alchemical treatise on Antimony, attributed to the Benedictine monk Basilius Valentinus with commentary by Theodor Kerckring.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Theodor Kerckring, Antimony, Spagyric Art, Alchemy, Benedictine Order</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory remarks of the author, establishing the spiritual and philosophical foundation required for the work. The catchword "In" from the previous page leads into the beginning of this discourse.</meta>

# The Triumphal Chariot

Before I rightly begin to describe and provide an account until the final completion, I have deemed it necessary to first offer a reminder of what is highly necessary for a God-fearing <term>Spagyrist</term> <note>original: "Spagiro"; a practitioner of alchemy who focuses on separating and recombining natural elements to create medicines</note> to know, and upon what he should build his rock. This is so that the houses may not be moved by the impetuous winds, much less be completely knocked into a heap, as those who otherwise build upon such rocks might have no certain foundation.

For I, as a <term>Cloister-man</term> <note>original: "Closter-Mann"; a monk or member of a religious order</note>, consider such things to be highly necessary—and they will likely remain highly necessary when I and you (be he <insert>Heinz or Kunz, Little Hans or Jack</insert> <note>German idioms for "any common person" or "everyman"</note>) are no longer seen here with eyes and have made our exchange with death. It is my wish that such a memorial should nevertheless remain after us in this sinful world, to the honor of God, so that His majesty and glory may be observed and noted, and that we may follow Him through sufficient <term>obedience</term> <note>original: "Parirung"; from the French 'parer', here meaning submission or compliance with divine will</note>. For my station requires a much different spirit than that of the common worldly station.

<summary>The author, writing from his perspective as a monk, emphasizes the necessity of a firm spiritual foundation for the alchemist, likening it to building a house upon a rock that can withstand the winds of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Spagyrics, alchemy, spiritual foundation, monastic life, mortality, divine glory, obedience</keywords>

# Of Antimony

->A 2<-

<margin>
Five points which are highly necessary for a God-fearing Spagyrist <note>A practitioner of spagyrics, the alchemical process of separating and recombining the essential components of plants and minerals.</note> to know.
</margin>

In my contemplations, I have actually found five points which are the most noble, and which all seekers of wisdom and lovers of the art are obliged to investigate. These are: first, the Invocation of God; second, the Contemplation of the Essence; third, the true and unadulterated Preparation; fourth, the Use; and fifth, the Utility. Every chemist and true alchemist must know how to consider and recognize these five points. For otherwise, without them, he cannot be perfect, nor can he be recognized as a true Spagyrist.

Therefore, we shall now begin and give a brief account of these points one after another, so that from this a complete work may come to light in a general and universal sense, and a perfect Work <note>original: "Opus"</note> may thereby be demonstrated.

<margin>
1. Invocation of God.
</margin>

The Invocation of God must occur through a heavenly devotion, from the depths of a pure, true heart and an unadulterated conscience.

<summary>The author outlines five essential pillars for the alchemical practitioner: prayer, contemplation of essence, correct preparation, usage, and utility. He emphasizes that spiritual devotion is the necessary starting point for any successful alchemical work.</summary>
<keywords>Spagyrics, Alchemist, Five Points of Alchemy, Invocation of God, Preparation of Antimony, Alchemical Ethics</keywords>

# The Triumphal Chariot

...earnestly (a) seeking and desiring through prayer: namely, a longed-for goal / thereafter

---

(a) Regarding what the *Author* <note>original: "Auctor"; likely referring to Basilius Valentinus</note> has extensively set forth here, and elsewhere frequently intermixes concerning piety, the worship of **GOD**, and the invocation of His name: I wish neither to praise nor to scold it. These may be signs of his inherited godliness; proofs of his sincerity; or they may be the marked traces and characteristics of a zeal often ignited through so many rambling and superfluous modes of speech. Thus, every man may hold this in high or low regard according to his own judgment and the convictions he has learned from his youth. My task was to translate the *Author* into *Latin* <note>The translator is explaining his process of rendering the original German into Latin for the scholarly world, though this text is the German edition.</note> in such a manner and order as I believed would least cause disgust to the delicate stomach of the reader. Furthermore, I wish to indicate in what way it seems useful for a true *Chemist* <term>Chymico: a practitioner of traditional alchemy</term> and *Spagyrist* <term>Spagiro: a follower of Paracelsus who uses alchemy to prepare medicines</term> not to neglect such matters. For since godliness is profitable for all things, as the Scripture teaches us, and the foremost exercise of godliness is prayer—through which heavenly goods are obtained from **GOD**, the giver of all good things—therefore *Basilius* <note>Basilius Valentinus, the purported 15th-century monk and alchemist</note> desires that one should lift one's mind to **GOD**, even in the midst of the burdensome chemical labors and duties. For even if one, through prayer, [obtains] nothing else...

<summary>This page contains a translator's note defending the spiritual tone of the original author, Basilius Valentinus, arguing that prayer and piety are essential tools for the practicing alchemist and spagyrist.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, piety, prayer, alchemy, spagyric medicine, chemical labor, religious devotion</keywords>

From this it follows that the mind falls upon so many things and takes heed of them, which certainly would not occur to the mind if it performed its work negligently and scattered among many distractions. And thus, through the help of prayer, we receive much which we gratefully acknowledge to have received from God. How often experience has taught this in <term>spagyric</term><note>Spagyric refers to the alchemical process of separating and recombining the essential components of a substance, usually plants or minerals, to create a more potent medicine.</note> labors is known to everyone who has ever undertaken such work with earnestness: how often he then suddenly receives that which he has long sought and could not find, and recognizes it as if it were poured into him from above, or given to him by a spirit. In the resolution of all hidden riddles, this is very useful; for when you burn, as it were, with a great desire to know them, this is prayer; and when you incline your mind to this or that, thinking back and forth and meditating much, this is the <term>co-operation</term><note>original: "Mitwürckung" — the human effort that must accompany divine grace.</note>; so that your prayer may not be in vain, nor tempt God. Yet all your undertaking is in vain as long as you do not find the <term>key</term><note>original: "Auffschluß" — literally an opening or unlocking; the breakthrough in understanding a secret process.</note>. Where you do not doubt, however, but in constant...

<summary>This page explores the psychological and spiritual dimensions of alchemy, arguing that intense focus and prayer lead to sudden divine insights or "keys" necessary for successful laboratory work. It emphasizes that human meditation and effort must cooperate with divine grace to solve the "riddles" of nature.</summary>

# Triumphal Chariot

<margin>
II.
Contemplation of the essence of every thing.
</margin>
Following this true invocation to dear God, there follows in due order the contemplation of every thing; that is to say, that everything must first be well-considered, namely the circumstances of every thing, what its <term>Matter and Form</term><note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "Matter" is the physical substance, while "Form" is the organizing principle or essence that makes a thing what it is.</note> are.

---

If you persist in your longing and do not cease to work, then finally, in a single moment, the insight will strike you. This is the revelation which would not have happened to you had you not prayed with the highest desire and worked with the application of the utmost diligence. And yet, you cannot comprehend how—out of all that you have thought, which was not yet the solution to the hidden riddle—the solution nevertheless emerged. The resolution of this hidden riddle will discover for you the greatest riddle of all: namely, what prayer is capable of in obtaining both spiritual and eternal, as well as physical and transitory goods. You will see that when prayer comes not from a hypocritical, but from a sincere and honest heart, nothing more useful can be found to obtain that which you desire. And let this be enough regarding prayer, which <term>Basilius</term><note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk whose writings emphasize the union of piety and laboratory work.</note> and all philosophers with him require as a prerequisite for <term>Chemistry</term><note>original: "Chymie"; here referring to Alchemy, the "Spagyric art" of separating and recombining substances.</note>; for: <note>original Latin: "pietas ad omnia utilis";</note> godliness is useful for all things, but especially for the very greatest.

<summary>This page outlines the transition from prayer to the intellectual contemplation of matter and form, emphasizing that divine revelation is the result of both intense spiritual longing and physical labor. It concludes by asserting that sincere piety is the essential prerequisite for success in the alchemical arts.</summary>

<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, alchemy, prayer, revelation, matter and form, contemplation, piety, spiritual labor</keywords>

# Triumphal Chariot

<margin>A Challenge to the Physicians.</margin>
Now I call here to me all the physicians of the world, and those who further undertake the practice of medicine—you who style yourselves Doctors, Masters, and Physicians of both medicines <note>original: "beyder Arzneyen" refers to the traditional qualification in both internal medicine and external surgery</note>—take good heed of your title of honor and consider your conscience: whether you hold such a title from God, or whether you use it only for the sake of appearance <note>original: "pro forma"</note> to enjoy high honors. # Of Antimony

The nature of things, and all that is related and belonging to the *aromatics* <note>original: "Aromatibus"; refers to fragrant or spicy substances used in alchemy and medicine</note>, should not be regarded or understood merely by their outward scent. Rather, it is the duty and responsibility of everyone who loves the true *philosophy* <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to Alchemy or the study of nature's hidden laws</note> to inquire and research what that scent actually is, from where it receives its power, and in what way its virtue can be proven useful. For it happens that a foul-smelling dung manures the earth, makes it fertile, and provides nourishment so that a noble, sweet-smelling fruit grows therefrom and comes to light. This occurs for many reasons, and to describe everything regarding the wondrous formation and the changing of natures from one into another would require the writing of a very large book.

<margin>*Digestion* and *putrefaction* <note>The processes of slow heating and decomposition</note> are two primary keys</margin>

# The Triumphal Chariot

A smith needs only a single fire, and iron is his only material, from which he arranges everything to make many kinds of things and instruments into their proper forms. Now he makes a roasting spit, soon a horseshoe, now a hook, soon something else, and so on; each is used for the purpose for which the master prepared it. Yet it was all the same metal and the same substance for such forged materials, which are nevertheless used for diverse purposes.

Likewise, from antimony <note>original: "Spießglas"; a historical term for the mineral stibnite, the primary ore of antimony</note> many kinds of manual work and many kinds of utility can be derived. The master is the smith who forms it; Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, used here as a <term>deckname</term> (code word) for the alchemical fire or the process of smelting</note> is the key to unlock it; the effect and the usefulness are brought to light by experience and practice.

See now, dear seeker of the Art, if you wish to become learned through my writings, and hope to attain wealth and to obtain the right, true medicine from Antimony <note>Antimony was a primary focus of late medieval alchemy, believed to hold the key to both the Philosopher's Stone and universal cures.</note>, then take this prescribed opinion well to heart. <margin>One poison attracts another to itself.</margin>
But before I make the virtue of **Antimony** <note>original: "Antimonii"; a metallic element used in alchemy for both purification and medicine</note> acceptable—since I have openly recognized and confessed that it is pure poison—I wish to present to the reader an example: that one poison attracts another, as its own kind, and can draw it to itself more than any foreign thing. For you, my reader, should mark, observe, and take good heed of this: namely, that the right, true, and unadulterated **Unicorn** <note>In the 17th century, "Unicorn horn" (often narwhal tusk) was a highly prized medicinal substance believed to detect and neutralize poisons</note> repels all poison and can by no means attract it. The proof is shown through this example:

<margin>Example of the Unicorn.</margin>
Take a spider that is alive; make a circle around it with the **Unicorn** [horn], and it will never be able to leave that circle; for it flees from that which resists poison. However, if a poisonous material of its own kind had been there, the spider would have felt no horror in overcoming the poison and passing through.

# The Triumphal Chariot

Just as fire, which lies hidden within its *matter* <note>original: "Materia"; the physical substance</note>, can accomplish nothing fruitful or enduring—so to speak—nor perform any active work unless it is first made manifest and proven: So it is also with all medicines. They cannot act until they are first separated from the coarse, *rectified* <note>purified by repeated distillation</note>, and thus liberated and *clarified* <note>cleared of impurities</note>, being proven and prepared so that all the world may recognize that the *separation of the pure from the impure* <note>original: "Separatio Puri ab Impuro"</note> has taken place. It is like the mountain being separated from the rich ore-metal; only then can a certain, unadulterated yield be hoped for and finally expected in truth. This cannot happen before the fire has first been opened and set free to work through the proper means.

*In summary* <note>original: "Summariter"</note>, I say this regarding the nature of *Antimony* <note>original: "Antimonium"; a metallic mineral central to the author's medical alchemy</note>: Everything that is hidden in secrecy is an *Art* <note>original: "Kunst"; implying a specialized, secret knowledge</note> as long as it remains hidden. However, as soon as that same secret is made manifest, the Art has reached its end and has become a *handicraft* <note>original: "Handwerck"; a common trade or manual skill</note>, as I have also [written] in my other books.

<summary>The author uses the metaphor of fire to explain that medicines, specifically antimony, must be purified and "opened" through alchemy to be effective. Definition of Antimony. <note>original: "Antimonii definitio"</note>
The Star of Antimony. <note>original: "Astrum Antimonii"</note>
</margin>
Now, **Antimony** <term>Antimonium; a metallic element often used in alchemy to purify gold</term> is an ore that has passed from its mountain vapors into a water. # Of Antimony

<margin>
The Calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile impurities or to oxidize it.</note> of Antimony.
</margin>

Take the very best Hungarian or other antimony <note>original: "Spießglas," a historical term for the mineral stibnite, the primary ore of antimony.</note> that you can obtain, and grind it small upon a stone, as finely as it can possibly be done, until it is imperceptible to the touch. When this is done, spread this finely ground **Antimony** very thinly upon a flat, broad, round or square earthen vessel, which has a rim all around it about two fingers high. Place this earthen vessel upon a calcining furnace; at first, make a gentle charcoal fire beneath it. When you see that the **Antimony** is beginning to smoke, you must stir the **Antimony** forward and back with a small iron rake, constantly and without any ceasing. Continue this until the **Antimony** no longer smokes in the slightest, nor can any vapor be perceived coming from it. Now, if it should happen during the calcination that the antimony begins to hang together and clump like knots, you should take note that you

Then lift the *Antimony* <note>original: "Antimonium"; a metallic element used in alchemy, often associated with purification</note> from the fire, let it become cold, and grind it again very small and fine. This process of stirring, grinding, and calcinating must be continued until the *Antimony*, as previously mentioned, gives off no more noticeable smoke, no longer clumps together, and has become whitish in color, like a pure white ash. Then the Calcination of the *Antimony* is rightly accomplished.

<margin>Vitrification <note>The process of turning a substance into glass</note></margin>
Afterward, take this calcinated *Antimony*, put it into a melting crucible—such as goldsmiths use in the fire for silver and gold—and place it before the bellows or in a blast furnace which must blow very strongly. Give it such a heat and fire that the calcinated *Antimony* flows well like water, quite clear and pure.

Therefore, it is much more reasonable to conclude: how much good would it not accomplish, and what diseases would it not heal, if the aforementioned healing power were freed from its harmful properties? Behold now such a **Glass of Antimony** <note>original: "Vitrum Antimonii"</note>, which I myself frequently use, and which can be used by anyone without any danger of a fatal outcome:

Take: Pure **Glass of Antimony**, made as Basilius <note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, the legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> teaches here; melt it in a crucible and let it flow until a third of it has been consumed. Grind this into an incredibly fine powder: then pour upon it the best rectified **Spirit of Wine** <note>original: "Spiritum Vini"; high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note> so that it stands three fingers high above it. Seal the vessel well and circulate the matter for three months. Then extract the **Spirit of Wine** through distillation, or if it is tinted a beautiful red (which always happens if you have proceeded correctly in your operation), simply pour it off and keep it separately, for it is an excellent medicine. Put the **Body** <note>original: "Corpus"; the solid remains of the substance</note> back into the crucible so that it melts, and pour it out into various molds; for it can take on all sorts of shapes, just as you desire,

# Triumphal Chariot

If the glass or marble has been previously warmed, put it once again into a new glass:

The procedure required for what remains is this: Pour distilled water over the above-mentioned pulp and draw it off again gently by means of a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "Balneum"; a vessel of hot water used to heat a container for gentle distillation</note>. If you repeat this for a third time, you will finally perceive that a sweet water comes over. This moment must be observed with the greatest possible diligence, for if this is missed, it is in vain and your labor is lost; you will obtain nothing but a <term>dead head</term> <note>original: "Caput mortuum"; the useless residue or dross left over after distillation or sublimation</note>. 

As soon as 20 or at most 30 drops of the sweet water have come over, an acidity follows again. Those who are either inexperienced or careless in this matter believe this to be the acidity of the vinegar; they therefore continue with their distillation and keep waiting for the water to become sweet. However, because this is an acidity of <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonii"</note> which immediately follows the sweet water after the vinegar has already been drawn out, it happens that by continuing the distillation, all the power and virtue of the antimony is destroyed, and only a useless and tasteless <term>dead head</term> remains behind. 

Therefore, be more cautious: as soon as this sweet water appears, take the pulp that is at the bottom and put it into another glass. Let it evaporate in the sun or by some other very gentle heat, so that a dry powder remains—

# Triumphal Chariot

### This (y) *medicine* performs even greater wonders, provided it is used correctly according to its proper order.

->The<-
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

---

(y) These medicines—which do not perform their effects in such a noticeable manner as those that purge, induce vomiting, or cause sweating and the like, but rather work imperceptibly by uniting the Universal Spirit <note>The "Universal Spirit" or Spiritus Mundi was believed by alchemists to be a vital force pervading the universe that could be captured in a medicinal tincture</note> with our own spirits, which they carry in a much purer form—improve nature and restore health. However, they are not to be used until the body has been unburdened of the refuse of harmful fluids <note>Refers to the "humors" of the body; the author argues for a preliminary cleansing before applying high alchemy</note>; otherwise, you would be throwing these jewels, as it were, into a dung-puddle, where, covered with filth, they would neither shine nor be able to prove their power. For although these could be called general medicines because of their many and varied virtues, they are to be categorized alongside the *topical* <note>original: "Topicis"</note> or external *medicaments* <note>original: "Medicamentis"</note>, in the use of which—according to the opinion of *Galen* <note>Claudius Galenus, a prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> and all other *physicians* <note>original: "Medicorum"</note>—the *general remedies* <note>original: "Generalia"</note> or universal treatments must precede and be used first. The *Tincture* spoken of here performs everything that *Basilius* <note>Basilius Valentinus, the legendary alchemist and monk to whom this work is attributed</note> relates, provided one persists with it for some time. For if this proverb holds true anywhere: *The use of medicine helps, but persistence with it heals* <note>original Latin: "Usus medicinae juvat, continuatio sanat"</note>; then this may certainly be said especially of those medicines which work imperceptibly.

# Of Antimony

<margin>Liquor of the Sulfur of Antimony</margin>
The aforementioned yellow powder—before it is extracted once more with the *Spirit of Wine* <note>original: "Spiritu Vini"; highly concentrated alcohol or ethanol</note>—should be ground fine upon a pre-heated stone. Take hard-boiled eggs (z) and slice them lengthwise, cutting them apart. Remove the yellow yolks and place this extracted yellow powder of the *Glass of Antimony* <note>original: "Vitro Antimonii"; a transparent, reddish-glass formed by roasting and melting antimony ore</note> into the hollows of the egg whites. This wonderful liquor heals all fresh wounds and open sores, provided it is used from the beginning and brushed into the wounds with a—

---

(z) One must always, as they say, handle soft eggs with caution and gentleness; however, regarding these hard eggs, I also have something to remind you of: namely, if you hurry during the removal of the yolk and tear that little membrane which separates the white from the yolk, you will see with astonishment how your balsam pulls itself into the white, thereby robbing you of a good portion of it.

# of Antimony

When such a **fermentation** <note>original: *Fermentatio*</note> has occurred—though I would indeed require much parchment to describe everything that occurs—to make mention of the most excellent tincture of all, I will for once teach the **Chemist** <note>original: *Chymicum*</note> what will be useful to him at all times in the future. Namely, that the greatest importance lies in knowing with what specific **solvent** <note>original: *Menstruo*; a term used in alchemy for a substance that dissolves a solid body</note> each particular tincture must be extracted. For it is not enough, as many falsely believe, that the **solvents** are sweet and possess no corrosive sharpness; rather, it is also necessary that they have a singular friendship and equality with the **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*; here referring to the "spirit" or essential liquid component of a substance</note> of that body over which they are poured, so that they may draw out the right and true sulfur from the same.

I wish to explain my meaning to you more clearly through an **example**. Distilled water draws out the tincture from the sulfur which has been prepared from **Glass of Antimony** <note>original: *Vitro Antimonii*; a transparent orange or red glass produced by roasting and melting antimony ore</note> by means of distilled vinegar. However, if you were to imagine that in such a manner the pure had been separated from the impure, you deceive yourself: for this water absorbs a certain salt which stains the tincture. Instead, rectified **Spirit of Wine** <note>original: *Spiritus Vini*; concentrated ethanol</note> is its proper **solvent**, which namely accepts only its volatile **sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*; in this context, the combustible or "soul" principle of the mineral</note>, whereas it has no affinity with the salt. This very same thing occurs with the tincture of coral, which is extracted with **Spirit of Wine** that has been distilled over **orange peels**; however, by this you obtain

# Of Antimony

I say this only to the physician: let him observe my proposals philosophically, undertake the work, and carry out the preparation for use. In doing so, he will daily find more praise for himself and learn to recognize more effects than any other physician could have previously prescribed for him.

   When you have brought the Antimony <term>Antimonium: a metallic element used in early medicine, often toxic if not properly refined</term> to this point and have succeeded correctly—in which you must act wisely and learn to consider the matter extensively through practical experience—then you may well boast of its mastery <term>Magisterium: a term for a master-preparation or a concentrated medicinal essence</term> which you have hunted down, and which is neither known nor common to many.

   And this mastery now will [have] powers mixed with the solution or tincture—

—resolved in a few hours into a Mercury <term>Mercurium: here referring to a liquid metallic essence rather than common quicksilver</term> united with Bacchus <note>original: "Baccho" - a poetic reference to wine or highly rectified spirits of wine (alcohol)</note>, whereby some dregs <term>Fæces: the solid residue left after distillation or filtration</term> remain behind. This mercurial solvent <term>Menstruum: a technical term for a solvent used to extract medicinal properties</term> is separated again from the dissolved gold; and in such a manner, you obtain an exceedingly heavy and weighty liquid <term>Liquorem</term>, which afterwards can never again be reduced to its former thickness and consistency. I have demonstrated this operation to several curious lovers of Chemistry. However, there will be an opportunity to speak of this elsewhere.

<margin>(kk) This union must occur so precisely that one part no longer separates from the other during distillation; otherwise, as the saying goes, all your oil, effort, and labor are lost. Those who best understand the great importance of this union in <term>Chemistry<note>original: "Chymie"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were often used interchangeably.</note></term> are those who merely pour two different things together and mix them, imagining wonders of what they might achieve. Later, however, when they find themselves deceived by experience, they first learn what a difference there is between a true and intimate union and a mere mixing together. For things that are simply mixed together gain no further powers than those that were already in them before; but through a true union, a certain special spirit or power—I know not what—is generated or brought into it, through which such things are effected that a man would never have believed could come from it.</margin>

# The Triumphal Chariot

The true *Arcanum* <note>A secret, powerful medicinal preparation</note> however remains united with the *Spirit of Wine* <note>original: "Spiritu Vini"; high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note> and *of Vitriol* <note>original: "Vitrioli"; sulfuric acid</note>; when you rectify <note>distill or purify</note> such an *Arcanum* once more, then a single drop is more powerful to use—taken with rose-water—than an entire pot of herbs boiled down. For the rising of the mother <note>original: "aufsteigenden Mutter"; a historical term for uterine hysteria or abdominal distress</note> and for *Colic*, it is a singular treasure to still them, which cannot be paid for with any amount of money, as this *Arcanum* proves its special merit therein.

<margin>*Elixir of Antimony*</margin>
Following the *Arcanum* of *Antimony* in order (II) is its Elixir; make it thus: Take, in the name of God, pure mineral *Antimony*, grind it small, and *sublimate* <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> it with half its weight of *Sal Ammoniac* <note>original: "Sal Armoniac"; ammonium chloride</note>. It might well cause a physician <note>original: "Artzt"</note> to wonder how this medicine could produce gentle bowel movements <note>original: "Sedes"; a common medical term for stools</note>, seeing as Antimony <note>original: "Antimonium"</note> is otherwise a piercing, tearing substance <note>original: "Materia"</note>, and Rhubarb <note>original: "Rhebarbar"</note> is added to it, which by itself <note>original: "per se"</note> also has a purgative power. ---

<margin>(mm) Note right well here: that the Galenic <note>referring to the traditional herbal medicine of Galen, which focused on balancing humors through evacuation</note> cathartics <note>original: "Cathartica"</note> or purgative medicines indeed have the power to carry away fluids, but not to improve them. The Chemical <note>original: "Chymischen"; referring to Alchemical or Spagyric preparations</note> medicines, however, are endowed with both properties. Certainly, it does not depend on how much is expelled, but rather on how much has been healed and improved: which the hidden power of Chemically-prepared medicines performs much better in a secret manner than that overt violence of purging.</margin>

Thus, they might recognize and take heed of the same wonders alongside me toward their Creator, and likewise be able to thank Him for them.

# 174 Triumphal Chariot

In order that I may further satisfy my intention—having resolved to describe the virtue of Antimony <note>original: "Antimonii"; a metallic element used in both metallurgy and medicine</note>, as far as I have attained it through my own experience—I shall rightly remain silent regarding those other matters which are still hidden from me and which I have not yet extracted from the stibnite <note>original: "Spießglas"; the natural mineral form of antimony sulfide</note>. For it would not befit me to pass judgment on foreign matters that lie outside my experience; instead, I commend them to another judge who has also learned something through his own diligence. Indeed, it is not possible for any man to fully master everything within Antimony, because the span of human life is short, and Antimony is very "round" <note>likely meaning "complete" or "cyclical" in an alchemical sense</note> and without end (as I have already explained previously), and something new is found within it every single day.

### The Use of its Weight / wherein the highest Masterpiece is contained.

If I were to allow a lengthy treatise to arise from my narrative, proving everything with detailed circumstances and examples, I would never finish; therefore, I will break off from this discourse and proceed further to another preparation, and record
<margin>On the fixity of Antimony.</margin>
the fixity <note>In alchemy, "fixity" refers to a substance's ability to withstand fire without evaporating or changing.</note> of Antimony. In this matter, it is much like wine: when its spirit is withdrawn and separated <term>separated: from the Latin 'separirt'</term> from the body, that same spirit heats the human body internally. When applied externally, it draws to itself all the causes of heat that have settled in inflamed limbs. On the other hand, when a sour vinegar has been prepared from the wine, that same vinegar conversely cools the body both inside and out. This occurs despite the fact that both—spirit and vinegar—are of the same genus and origin, born from a single root. The reason for this, and for your instruction, is that the preparation of vinegar occurs solely through digestion <term>digestion: a process of slow, gentle heating in a sealed vessel</term>, whereby a putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the chemical breakdown or 'rotting' of a substance to release its essence</term> of the wine follows,

Take Antimony <note>original: "Spießglas," the mineral stibnite</note>, as much as the artist desires; grind it very small and fine. When you have such a fine powder, put it into a glass flask <note>original: "Kolben-Glas"</note>, and pour upon it a strong *Nitric Acid* <note>original: "Aquafort" or Aqua Fortis</note>, so that it stands six inches above it, according to the measuring rod. Afterward, pour off this *extraction* quite cleanly and purely, and filter it, so that all *dregs* <note>original: "Feces"</note> and all impurities are removed from it.

# The Triumphal Chariot

<note>The header refers to the "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony," a famous alchemical work.</note>

Followers of the true **spagyric art** <note>spagyric art: an early term for alchemy, specifically the process of separating and recombining the essential components of plants and minerals</note> will consider my writing more favorably than those who seek only to display their own malicious envy. The latter then writes in the 8th chapter concerning **minerals** as follows: "That which is of lesser value, God allows to be revealed; but that which is of greater importance remains generally hidden until the coming of *Elias the Artist*" <note>original: "Eliæ Artistæ." A legendary figure in alchemy predicted to reveal all secrets of the art at the end of time.</note> (others read it as) "until the art of *Helias* <note>original: "Heliæ." Likely a variation of Elias, sometimes associated with Helios/the Sun.</note> when he shall arrive." And again, in the book *On Minerals* <note>original: "de Mineralibus"</note> in the first treatise, he says: "It is indeed true that much still lies hidden in the earth which is unknown to me as it is to others. For this I know: that God will hereafter reveal many wonderful things, and that much more will be brought to the light of day than has been known to all of us before this time. This is certainly true: there is nothing hidden that shall not be discovered. Therefore, one will come after me whose **Great Work** <note>original: "Magnale." Refers to a work of great or divine magnitude.</note> and excellent being does not yet live at this time, who will reveal much." Be of good cheer, therefore, you lover of the **chemical art**, and prepare the way for this *Helias*, who will bring with him more fortunate times; in those days he will reveal more than our ancestors—due to the malice and resentment prevalent in their time—ever dared to discover. Whoever you may be...

<margin>Flowers of Antimony <note>original: "Flores Antimonii." In early chemistry, "flowers" refers to a powdery solid produced by sublimation—heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again.</note></margin>
However, so that I may complete my report by describing the Flowers of Antimony, the art-loving disciple should know that the true flowers of <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Spießglas," an old name for the mineral stibnite or antimony</note> can be prepared not in one way, but in many ways, manners, and paths, as is known to every true <term>spagyrist</term> <note>original: "Spagiro," a practitioner of alchemical medicine who separates and recombines substances to purify them</note>. Others have <term>instruments</term> <note>original: "Inſtrumenta"</note> specially formed and prepared for this purpose, with hollow wind-pipes so that the antimony can have air to rise upward.

Yet again, others perform the <term>sublimation</term> <note>the process of turning a solid directly into a vapor and back to a solid</note> with a strong fire, having placed three <term>alembics</term> <note>original: "Helmen," or "helmets," the caps of a distilling apparatus</note> one above the other, making white, yellow, and red flowers all at once; for which reason I have followed in like manner and likeness, so that I have never given birth to any failure therein.

The new disease of the soldiers <note>A common 17th-century reference to syphilis, often called the "French disease" or "camp fever"</note> recently scattered among us in these times, is thereby consumed and dried out; it performs wonder upon wonder, provided only that the physician understands its use in—

—then it will rise over the helm <note>The top part of a distilling apparatus, also called an alembic</note> as deep red as blood. Then draw off the spirit of wine <note>term: Spiritum Vini; concentrated alcohol or ethanol used as a solvent</note> from it once more, and you will have a thick, heavy oil.

How this oil is subsequently mixed and united with its salt, we shall report elsewhere; as its greatest utility consists in the improvement of metals <note>A reference to the alchemical transmutation of base metals into gold or silver</note>. Here we have described it and brought it to the point that it has become an excellent medicine for the most dangerous diseases, which are commonly held to be incurable. It performs all its effects through sweat. And since there is now no disease which it does not come to aid, what need is there to enumerate any of them? It is better to remain entirely silent than to diminish its praise by the recounting of one or another. # Of Antimony

as such was also practiced in long and very ancient times by our ancestors, providing the opportunity at hand. Therefore, note especially that it is better to take **Salt of Tartar** <note>original: "Sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate derived from cream of tartar</note> instead of raw **Cream of Tartar** <note>original: "Wein-Stein"; crude potassium bitartrate</note>, not only for the sake of shortening the operation (as one would otherwise have to melt it for a long time until the Cream of Tartar was changed into salt), but primarily because one obtains a much more highly-colored glass. 

**Basilius** <note>referring to Basilius Valentinus, the legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> also forgets, or omits for certain reasons to mention, that the glass must be poured into a previously warmed basin and then crushed in a mortar that has likewise been previously warmed; otherwise, because of the **Salt of Tartar**, this glass would very quickly be changed into a mush or paste as soon as it felt the air. 

Furthermore, he also leaves out indicating the quantity of water in which the previously crushed powder must be dissolved, so that one may obtain a more beautiful and complete color, in the enhancement of which its principal power and virtue consists. Moreover, he also passes over in silence the **precipitation** <note>the process of a substance settling out of a solution</note> of the **Liver** <note>original: "Leber"; likely Liver of Antimony, a sulfurous mixture</note> in the water, which must be done with vinegar; however, both this and what has already been mentioned before are very necessary parts of this operation.

There are also many kinds of oils made from <term>Antimony</term><note>original: "Spießglas"; the common early modern term for the mineral stibnite</note>, some by themselves or *per se* and without any addition, but others—and many varieties—with additions. And I shall now tell you this parable: that many kinds of animals are found which sustain themselves solely in the earth, such as many creeping creatures like worms and snakes and other species, of which sometimes new births arise out of <term>putrefaction</term><note>original: "Fäulung"; the alchemical process of decomposition believed to be the precursor to new life or transformation</note> that were never recognized before. # Of Antimony

Take the <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: "Spießglas"; stibnite or raw antimony ore</note> entirely raw and grind it small; put it into a glass <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for distillation</note> with a flat bottom, and pour upon it the right, true <term>Vinegar of the Philosophers</term> <note>a specialized alchemical solvent, often distilled vinegar or a secret acid</note>, which vinegar has been sharpened with its own salt. Seal the <term>Cucurbit</term> very firmly and well, and place it in <term>Horse-Dung</term> <note>original: "Roß-Mist" or "Fimum equinum"; used as a source of gentle, steady heat</note>, or in the <term>Bath of Our Lady</term> <note>original: "Bad unser lieben Frauen" or "Balneum Mariae"; a water bath or double boiler</note>, and let it <term>putrefy</term> <note>rot or break down through gentle heat</note> for forty days. Thus the <term>Body</term> <note>original: "Corpus"</note> will open itself, and the vinegar will become blood-red. Pour off that same vinegar and pour other vinegar upon it until it will color itself no more. Then filter the vinegar until clear through a felt or coarse paper; put it again into a clean glass as before, and set it there once more, well-closed and sealed, to <term>putrefy</term> for forty days as previously, in the <term>Water Bath</term> or <term>Horse-Dung</term>. Thus the <term>Body</term> opens itself anew, and the <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materia"</note> in the glass becomes as black as ink or shoemaker's blacking. When you have this sign, then the true <term>Solution</term> <note>original: "Solutio"</note> is completed, through which the separation of the elements can further take place.

# of Antimony <note>original: "Antimonii"</note>

take heed, and reflect upon the fundamental reasons of which I now speak and write.

When a person considers the wretched, miserable state of our life, into which we have come through the Fall of our first parents, and because of Original Sin <note>original: "Erb-Sünde"</note>—which was inherited by us through that Fall—we are subjected to so much suffering <note>literally "cross," referring to the burdens of life</note>, tribulation, anxiety, and distress; indeed, we are often burdened with diseases and manifold infirmities. If one truly considered this, he would apply greater diligence and use his time to seek the salvation and welfare of his neighbor through Christian Love. This duty is commanded and imposed upon him by the high Prince of Heaven as a matter of obligation. 

However, because the greatest and largest multitude waste and spend their time with other useless matters, and because labor seems too bitter and difficult for them, they remain stuck in their old outward appearances. They let the essence <note>literally "the flesh"</note> slip away so that they do not have to grease their fingers or soil their hands; they act as if they fear a national shortage might arise at the grocers' <note>original: "Krämern"</note> regarding the supply of soap.

# Of Antimony

That in antimony, or from antimony, such a Mercury can be found—nor can the quicksilver or <term>Primum Ens<note>Latin: "First Essence" or the primordial matter of all things.</note></term> and first water of the metals be prepared—so perfectly that the Great Stone of the ancient Sages <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy.</note> might be made from it; rather, this <term>Primum Ens</term> and such a seed is found in another mineral: wherein, according to its metallic nature, the effect is found to be even higher than in antimony. And I wish for nothing more to be lacking than this alone: that the artist learn to distinguish the nature of metals and minerals, as well as quite diligently observe and take heed of the preparation of antimony, as well as its use; and then the complete final judgment can follow thereafter, and not before.

# The Triumphal Chariot

Therefore, I wish to further fulfill and grant my promise and my consolation, and henceforth share diligent instruction with my disciples according to their desire, so that the final, true separation of the good from the evil can be observed and recognized. <margin>The Vinegar of the Philosophers from Antimony.</margin>
The ore from which the Antimony is smelted and purely refined must be ground very subtly and small. Place this same ground material into a round glass vessel, which is called a <term>vial</term> <note>original: Phiole</note>, with a very long neck. Pour distilled rain-water upon it until the glass is half-filled. Set it, well-sealed, into <term>horse-manure</term> <note>original: Roß-Mist; used as a consistent, low-heat source for digestion</note> to <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: putrificiren; the process of breaking down or "rotting" a substance to release its inner essence</note>, until the ore begins to ferment and a foam begins to rise to the top. Then it is time to take it out, as this is a sign that the <term>body</term> <note>original: Corpus; the physical matter of the mineral</note> has opened itself.

# The Triumphal Chariot

<margin>The powers of the Vinegar of the Philosophers. <note>original: "Vires Aceti Philosophorum"</note></margin>
This vinegar cools excellently and well—not in the common manner of another ordinary vinegar, but with sufficient cause for wonder and great experience. It is especially effective in stilling <term>gangrene</term> <note>original: "kalten Brandes," literally "cold fire," referring to necrotic tissue or gangrene</note> that has been ignited by gunpowder. It also stills other angry injuries and limbs when it is rubbed together with the <term>Soul of Saturn</term> <note>original: "Anima Saturni," an alchemical term usually referring to the acetate of lead</note> and made into an <term>ointment</term> <note>original: "Unguento"</note> for external application.

Mixed with endive water, and with the addition of burnt-out, fixed, common <term>saltpeter</term> <note>potassium nitrate</note> made with sulfur, it disperses <term>the quinsy</term> <note>original: "Bräune," an old term for severe throat inflammation or tonsillitis</note>, extinguishes its mighty heat, and stills the inflamed blood.

Used in times of <term>pestilence</term> <note>the plague</note> and taken internally—about a spoonful at a time—and applied over the plague-boils with linen cloths, it draws out the poison and cools excellently. But take note: if you wish to use it externally in this way, mix it with a third part of water that has been burnt and distilled from the <term>bodies of frogs</term> <note>original: "Leichen der Frösche"; frog spawn or frog water was a common cooling remedy in early modern pharmacy</note>.

# Of Antimony

<margin>
Whether the Signet-Star of *Antimony* <note>original: "Antimonii"</note> is the Matter of the *Philosophers' Stone*? <note>original: "Lap. Phil."</note>
</margin>

Many people have held the Signet-Star of Antimony <note>original: "Spießglas," an old name for the mineral stibnite or antimony</note> in very high and great esteem; and many people have exerted themselves excellently and very highly to create such a Star, so that many artists have spared no diligence to bring it to pass, which some have indeed succeeded in doing. For others, however, their undertaking sank into the depths of the water <note>a metaphor for failure or loss of material during the process</note>. And almost all were of the opinion that this Star was the true *Matter* <note>original: "Materia"</note> from which the *Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"</note> could be made. Based on this opinion and their imagination—because Nature herself had marked it for them with a Star—they believed their thoughts could not be led astray onto any false path.

But I say no to this; for they wander far away, such seeking men, from the right path of the paved road, and they climb too high upon the crags and stone cliffs where the chamois and ibex have their dwellings and the birds of prey take their flight. It has not been granted to this Star to find such power within it, nor to make such a noble Stone out of it.

How the Signature-Star is made.

Repeat this for the third time, and the Regulus will purify itself and become beautifully clean. # Of Antimony

I have wished to leave behind this beginning, so that my succeeding students may not require so much time for their initial experiences, and that they might reach the desired goal all the sooner, giving thanks to me for this—next to GOD.

<margin>The Solution of Crystals, for the Bladder Stone.</margin>
In this oil, yet another great miracle is found, namely: that after a long period of circulation <note>A process of continuous distillation where the liquid returns to the base of the vessel</note>, this oil attacks crystals—provided they have first been calcined <note>Reduced to powder by heat</note> for three days and nights—and draws the salt out of them. When that has occurred, and the oil is driven over once more through a retort <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note>, one then possesses such a powerful medicine that it miraculously grinds down and expels the bladder stone <note>Calculi or kidney stones</note>, and effectively performs many other things besides.

Now, to mention something also regarding the Lead of the Philosophers <note>original: "Bley der Philosophen"; a metaphorical term for the purified essence of a base metal, here referring to Antimony</note>: everyone who desires to investigate nature should know that Antimony is very closely related and devoted to common lead. It is in the same form and manner as a tree which pushes out from its sides its excess resin <note>original: "Resinæ"</note>, which is the sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the principle of combustibility and soul</note> of the tree.

# APPENDIX.

Finally, understand also that Antimony <note>original: "Spießglas," a common historical term for the mineral stibnite or raw antimony</note> is used for many other things as well: such as for the typefaces <note>original: "Schrifften"</note> used in printing houses.

Also, under certain constellations and alignments of the planets, a mixture of metals is made with Antimony, from which seals and characters <note>talismanic symbols or planetary sigils</note> are cast, which are said to possess special virtues; likewise, mirrors are prepared from such a mixture, possessing many strange visions and properties.

Also, small bells and large bells are cast from it, which have a wonderful tone, as well as human figures and many other things besides. (qqq)

---

(qqq) The powers and virtues of Antimony, which the Author in this appendix only touches upon and skips over as if in passing, are so many and varied that not even the hundredth part of them is yet known to people.

But because such things are not related or akin to the art of medicine <note>original: "Artzney"</note> / also my—

<column-break/>

which ignorance, without doubt / results in the shame and mockery of our race / that we humans / who alone among so many animals are endowed with reason and the power of rational deduction / are driven with such force and desire toward a sorrowful and loathsome greed for profit / that almost no one has the time / to investigate the aforementioned wonders / which the Author of Nature has implanted in His creatures; however, I do not wish to repeat this song of lamentation so often struck up by *Valentinus* <note>Basilius Valentinus, the purported 15th-century alchemist and author of this text</note> / but this alone you must well consider: This mineral <note>Antimony</note> has such a wonderful spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; referring to the volatile or essential essence of the substance</note> hidden within it / that it cannot be exhausted through extraction / and moreover possesses such powers / which can never be sufficiently studied by anyone.

What I have experienced outside of chemistry <note>original: "Chymie"</note> and medicine / is indeed little; yet experience has taught me such things / that I do not hold / that *Antimony* <note>A metallic element used in alchemy for both transmutation and medicine</note> will show itself to be any less wonderful in other matters / than it has already proven itself to be in chemistry and medicine.
